# Slides Skill

Use this skill as reference material when creating or editing presentation slide decks.

## Important Instructions

- [HARD REQUIREMENT] Content quality and storytelling: before planning the deck, read and follow [Content Quality and Narrative Rules](references/content-rules.md). Ensure the deck covers everything the user requested and forms a coherent, audience-appropriate narrative rather than a collection of disconnected facts.
- [HARD REQUIREMENT] Audience-facing copy: visible slide content must be written for the intended audience, not for the person or model producing the deck. Do not expose planning notes, timing scaffolds, talk tracks, content-selection commentary, or other internal process language unless the user explicitly requests it.

- Info density: avoid cramming low-value details onto a single slide. Prefer lower-density slides with high-value content.
  - Title slide: keep the title slide minimal and simple. Avoid cramming in too much information.
- Layout: keep things clean and simple. Avoid low-quality visuals, but also avoid excessive white space. By default, use equal left and right margins on each slide.
- [HARD REQUIREMENT] Overlap: always pay attention to programmatic overlap warnings. Do not assume that overlapping elements in diagrams are intentional, and do not ignore overlap warnings without inspecting them. You MUST fix all unintended overlap errors before delivering the slides. This is critical.
- [HARD REQUIREMENT] Font size: when a template is provided, match its font sizes. When no template or style guidance is given, you MUST use at least 50pt for deck titles, 35pt for slide titles, 24pt for mid-level text such as subheadings, callout headers, and text-box titles, and 16pt for body text.
- Text layout: when there is too much text, shorten it before shrinking the font size. Inspect visually for unexpected text wrapping. NEVER allow a title/banner text box intended for one line to wrap to two lines.
- Narrative copy must fit the chosen layout: shorten it or change layouts rather than adding density or shrinking type.
- Visual assets:
  - [HARD REQUIREMENT] DO NOT use Python to draw images; DO NOT use programmatic vector shapes for visuals; DO NOT use programmatic drawings of any sort. Use image search or image_gen tool instead!
  - [HARD REQUIREMENT] Minimize the use of diagrams. Add them only when requested or when a single diagram materially improves the clarity of complex concepts. Diagram implementation rules: use native PowerPoint shapes for simple diagrams; use Graphviz for complex relational/topological/network-like diagrams; use image_gen for highly aesthetic, illustrative, or scientific infographic diagrams (e.g. chemical structures, circuit diagrams, etc.). When using native PowerPoint shapes with connectors, create connectors (arrows/edges) before creating entity nodes, so edges appear behind nodes and never cross through node shapes or labels. If this ordering is awkward during early iteration, you may create nodes first in the initial draft, then switch to connectors-first in the revised code.
  - Before sourcing or generating visuals, be mindful of the desired aspect ratio, placement, and cropping options on the slide. For example, if you intend to place text to the left of the image containing a person, you should ask image_gen to put the person on the right side of the image.
  - By default, DO NOT reuse the same image more than once (unless it's a background).
  - Prepare visuals for both the main concept and decorative support.
- Default styling: use one composition instead of a collection of UI panels. UI-like styling typically includes card grids, pills, badges, button-like text boxes, tab or navigation patterns, repeated modular panels, dense dashboard-style layouts, and other component-library aesthetics that imply interactivity. Use stylized text boxes sparingly, favoring a flat structure on the canvas.

## Skill Folder Contents

Contents of the `slides/` skill folder:

- `container_tools/`: Standalone python scripts for slides and relevant asset manipulation.
- `references/`: Additional workflow references for specialized presentation tasks.
- `template_following_scripts/`: Helper scripts for exact source-deck/template following.
- `artifact_tool/`: API documentation and coding examples for the artifact tool library.
- `builtin_templates_support/`: Checked-in guidance, manifests, prompts, and reusable scripts for built-in templates. Each template owns its `ARTIFACT.md`; shared runners live once under `builtin_templates_support/scripts/`.
- `assets/builtin_templates/codex-grid-layout-library/`: Blob-managed static assets for the built-in Codex Grid template, including 26 rendered previews, a model-facing registry, structured content tokens, and 26 exact plain-JavaScript artifact-tool Compose reconstructions with no JSX. This directory contains no Markdown, prompts, or reusable runners.

## Container Tools

The following helper scripts are located in the `container_tools/` directory:

- `ensure_raster_image.py`: Ensure images are rasterized; convert to PNG if needed; quick usage `--input_files <img_path1> ...`.
- `render_slides.py`: Render a PowerPoint file into a folder of PNG slides using default sizing; quick usage: `<input.pptx>`. Output files are named `slide-1.png`, `slide-2.png`, ... in a directory with the same name as the input file.
- `create_montage.py`: Build a tiled montage from images in a directory (for viewing multiple image assets or rendered slides at once); quick usage: `--input_dir <imgs_dir> --output_file <montage.png>`. It supports most image formats with auto conversion under the hood.
- `slides_test.py`: Detect content overflowing the original slide canvas; usage: `<input.pptx>`.

# Content Quality and Narrative Rules

Read this file before planning or authoring a presentation.

## Define the communication job

Before selecting slides, layouts, or visuals, infer from the request and context:

- The intended audience; do not assume the audience is the user.
- The deck's job: educate, persuade, sell, recommend, facilitate, or enable a
  decision.
- The audience outcome: what they should understand, believe, choose, approve,
  discuss, or do.
- The central takeaway and the essential claims or evidence needed to support it.
- Every content requirement and source constraint in the user's request.

Express the communication job in one sentence:

> By the end, **[audience]** should **[outcome]** because **[central takeaway]**.

For an exploratory, reference, or neutral deck, define the questions or
understanding the audience should leave with rather than forcing a recommendation.

## Choose an appropriate narrative arc

Choose a structure that fits the communication job, such as:

- Context -> stakes -> evidence -> implications -> action.
- Question -> analysis -> answer.
- Problem -> causes or options -> recommendation.
- Current state -> change -> future state.
- Chronology, process, learning progression, or claim -> evidence -> consequence.

An agenda is not by itself a narrative. Make the sequence cumulative so each
section creates the need for the next. Do not force a persuasive or emotional arc
onto a technical or reference deck; build those through logic, causality,
chronology, learning progression, or decision flow.

## Make every slide advance the story

- Give each slide one narrative job and one primary claim.
- Prefer takeaway-style titles that communicate the point rather than merely
  naming the topic.
- Order slides so each answers a question raised by the prior slide or introduces
  one the next slide resolves; add a transition when the logic would otherwise
  jump.
- Integrate requested topics into the throughline, and remove repeated beats,
  redundant summaries, and content that does not advance it.
- When following a template, preserve its title and layout conventions; strengthen
  the narrative through sequencing and copy rather than breaking the template.

## Turn evidence into meaning

Do not present facts, metrics, charts, examples, or visuals as an inventory. For
each important piece of evidence, show what it means, why it matters to this
audience, and how it changes the conclusion, decision, discussion, or next step.

Charts and visuals must reinforce the current claim. Use grounded examples,
scenarios, case studies, or human impact only when they materially improve
understanding. Never invent people, quotes, facts, data, or outcomes.

## Open and close deliberately

Open with the context, purpose, stakes, question, or tension that makes the deck
worth attention. Close by resolving that opening with the appropriate outcome: a
recommendation or decision, an explicit action, a synthesis or application, or
implications and productive questions. Do not end abruptly on a detail, technical
artifact, unframed summary, or generic "Thank you" slide.

## Write natural, audience-facing copy

Visible copy must serve the intended audience. Do not expose model instructions,
planning notes, timing scaffolds, talk tracks, or implementation details unless the
user explicitly requests them.

Use a requested duration to set scope and pacing; show timings only when explicitly
requested or audience-facing, otherwise keep them in speaker notes. Remove anything
the user marks irrelevant or out of scope; state exclusions only when the audience
needs them or the user asks.

Write titles and body copy in direct, specific, natural language:

- Prefer concrete claims, active verbs, and plain language over vague slogans,
  jargon, abstract noun clusters, or inflated phrasing.
- Avoid formulaic constructions such as repeated "From X to Y" titles, punctuation
  patterns, parallel structures, or advertising-style fragments.
- Remove duplicated qualifiers and concepts, and vary sentence structure naturally.

A title should communicate a point a human presenter would plausibly say aloud. If
it sounds like a prompt, production note, slogan generator, or mechanical template,
rewrite it.

# Plan Review ??HTML Visual Editor ??PPTX Pipeline

**Verdict: the plan is feasible, and it is a better plan than the one it replaces.** Every capability the user asked for exists in `python-pptx`. What needs attention is not feasibility ??it's four traps that each silently produce a wrong-looking deck, and one scoping consequence the user may not have noticed.

**Grounding note.** Code execution was denied this session (`python`, `unzip`, .NET `ZipFile`, `Expand-Archive` ??all gated). This is the *third* consecutive stage to hit it. So rather than assert `python-pptx` behaviour from memory, I **read the installed source** and cite `file:line` for every API claim. The claims are grounded; nothing was executed end-to-end.

## 1. Headline findings

### F1 ??This plan supersedes the slide-cloning workstream. Delete it.

`renderer_draft_brief.md` specifies an `lxml` slide-cloning engine. It exists to **reuse hand-designed slides**. A human drawing from scratch doesn't need to reuse a design ??they need the slide *chrome*: logo, divider, page number. **That chrome lives in the layout, not the slides**, and arrives free with a plain `add_slide()`.

From `layout_manifest.json`, layout index 3 (`2_?ъ슜??吏???덉씠?꾩썐` ??the layout 15 of the 23 template slides already use) carries `decoration_shapes` = `洹몃┝ 3` (logo), `洹몃옒??8` (divider graphic), `?щ씪?대뱶 踰덊샇 媛쒖껜 ? 5` (page number), plus placeholder idx 10 (BODY, `0.6327, 0.3715`, `10.0734 횞 0.5174 in`, 30pt) ??the title header.

That is *precisely* the "Title Header + boundary + body" structure the user described.

```python
prs = Presentation("Vieworks_R&D_ppt_Template.pptx")
slide = prs.slides.add_slide(prs.slide_layouts[3])   # logo + divider + page number, free
slide.placeholders[10].text_frame.text = title       # on-brand, correctly positioned
# ...then draw the user's shapes absolutely.
```

This removes the highest-risk module in the project. **One caveat codex-critic must rule on:** Q3 blocked `add_slide()` because the template's designs live in slide-level freeform shapes rather than semantic placeholders. That objection is about *reusing designs*. It does not apply to *drawing your own design onto layout chrome*, which is all this plan does. I believe the Q3 block doesn't bind here ??but it was codex-critic's call originally, so it should be codex-critic's call to lift.

### F2 ??The red line must not be exported.

The layout already draws the real divider (`洹몃옒??8`). If the exporter also emits the user's red line, **the deck ships two divider lines, stacked**. The red line is editor chrome, not content ??draw it as a CSS `::after` on the stage so it is structurally incapable of reaching the renderer.

Corollary the user should hear: the editor shows a flat red line, PowerPoint shows the real gradient. **The header region is deliberately not WYSIWYG.** 짠6 fixes that for free.

### F3 ??"Century Gothic for both English and Korean" is not achievable.

Century Gothic ships no Hangul glyphs. No API can conjure them. Verified in source:

- `Font.name` writes **only** `<a:latin>` ??`text/text.py:363-369` ??`get_or_add_latin()`.
- `CT_TextCharacterProperties` (`oxml/text.py:263-315`) declares `latin` and `hlinkClick` and **no `a:ea` element at all**. The API cannot express an East-Asian typeface; you must drop to lxml.
- And if you *did* force `ea="Century Gothic"`, PowerPoint finds no Hangul glyph and substitutes anyway.

The honest contract is a **split**: `latin = "Century Gothic"`, `ea = "留묒? 怨좊뵓"`. Then mirror it in CSS: `font-family: 'Century Gothic', 'Malgun Gothic', sans-serif;`

This is the pleasing part: **the browser's font-fallback chain and PowerPoint's latin/ea split are the same mechanism.** Configure them consistently and preview and export agree ??the only route to WYSIWYG for mixed KO/EN. Chase "Century Gothic for Korean" and they diverge forever.

```python
from pptx.oxml.ns import qn

def set_run_fonts(run, latin: str, ea: str) -> None:
    rPr = run._r.get_or_add_rPr()
    rPr.get_or_add_latin().typeface = latin
    ea_el = rPr.find(qn("a:ea"))
    if ea_el is None:
        ea_el = rPr.makeelement(qn("a:ea"), {})
        # a:ea must follow a:latin and precede a:cs/a:sym/a:hlink... per the DrawingML schema.
        rPr.insert_element_before(
            ea_el, "a:cs", "a:sym", "a:hlinkClick", "a:hlinkMouseOver", "a:rtl", "a:extLst"
        )
    ea_el.set("typeface", ea)
```
(`insert_element_before` verified at `oxml/xmlchemy.py:685`.)

Two more font facts to surface **before** the user commits:
- **Century Gothic is not the Vieworks brand font.** `exemplar_catalog.md` shows the template's real design fonts are **酉곗썚??B** and **酉곗썚??M**, set at run level on every slide. Century Gothic body text will sit next to inherited 酉곗썚??chrome and look off-brand. The user's call ??but it should be a decision, not an accident.
- **If Century Gothic isn't installed on the opening machine, PowerPoint substitutes and geometry shifts.** `python-pptx` cannot embed fonts. Low risk, invisible until someone else opens the deck.

### F4 ??`add_shape()` hands you Office blue, not your colour.

`new_autoshape_sp` (`oxml/shapes/autoshape.py:249-287`) hardcodes a `<p:style>` block pointing `lnRef`, `fillRef` and `effectRef` at `schemeClr accent1`, and `fontRef` at `lt1`. **`accent1` in this template is `#4472C4`** ??stock Office blue (`layout_manifest.json:42`). So every shape arrives blue-filled, blue-outlined, **drop-shadowed**, with white theme-font text. Four overrides required, and the third is the one everyone forgets:

```python
shp.fill.solid(); shp.fill.fore_color.rgb = RGBColor.from_string(hex)
shp.line.color.rgb = RGBColor.from_string(stroke)
shp.shadow.inherit = False        # kills effectRef ??verified dml/effect.py:33-41
set_run_fonts(run, "Century Gothic", "留묒? 怨좊뵓")   # + run.font.color
```

Without `shadow.inherit = False`, every flow-chart box carries an Office drop shadow that no amount of fill/line overriding removes. There is no other shadow API.

Good news in the same XML: `<a:bodyPr anchor="ctr">` and `<a:pPr algn="ctr">` are the defaults, so autoshape text is **already middle/center-aligned** ??exactly what a flex-centered HTML div does. That lines up for free.

## 2. Q1 ??Frontend?뭕ackend contract

**JSON, absolute coordinates, in a defined model-unit space. Not percentages.**

The load-bearing number: the slide is `12192000 횞 6858000` EMU (13.3333 횞 7.5in, 16:9).

**Use a 960 횞 540 model space.** Then `12192000/960 = 12700`, `6858000/540 = 12700`, and `914400/72 = 12700`. So **1 model unit ??1 point ??exactly 12700 EMU** ??integer, exact, zero drift. And the payoff that matters most: **font sizes map 1:1**, a 14-unit font is a 14pt font, no scale factor anywhere. This one choice deletes the entire coordinate-and-font-scale bug class before it exists.

(1280횞720 also divides exactly ??9525 EMU/px ??but then fonts need a 횞0.75 factor. 960횞540 is strictly better. Decouple model space from display: keep the model at 960횞540, zoom with `transform: scale()` on the stage. Never store device pixels.)

**Why not percentages:** they force float math on every read, make font size ambiguous (percent of *what*?), and throw away exact-integer reproducibility for nothing. Absolute-in-model-units is already resolution-independent, because the unit is *defined* rather than tied to a device.

```jsonc
{
  "schema": "canvas-ir/1",
  "canvas": { "w": 960, "h": 540, "unit": "pt" },   // echo -> backend hard-fails on mismatch
  "target": { "template": "Vieworks_R&D_ppt_Template.pptx", "layout_index": 3 },
  "title":  "AF Camera Multi Exposure",              // -> placeholder idx 10, NOT a new textbox
  "elements": [                                      // array order IS z-order
    { "id": "e1", "type": "rect",
      "x": 100, "y": 120, "w": 180, "h": 60, "rotation": 0,
      "fill": "#FF0032", "stroke": { "color": "#FFFFFF", "width": 1 },
      "text": { "runs": [ { "t": "Capture", "size": 14, "bold": true, "color": "#FFFFFF" } ],
                "align": "ctr", "valign": "ctr" } }
  ]
}
```

- **Version it.** This IR *will* change.
- **Echo the canvas dims** so the backend rejects a stale payload rather than silently rescaling it into a subtly-wrong deck.
- **Array order is z-order.** A separate `z` field is a second source of truth and will desync.
- **The title maps to inherited placeholder idx 10, not a free textbox** ??on-brand and correctly positioned for free. In the editor, **lock** the title box to `x 45.6, y 26.8, w 725.3, h 37.3` (model units) so it cannot drift from the master.

**Transport: do both.** (1) Local FastAPI, HTML served same-origin, `POST /api/export` ??`.pptx` download ??the user's "Export PPT" button. (2) **A CLI fallback that always works**: "Download JSON" plus `python -m aippt.render_canvas deck.json -o out.pptx`. Corporate IT frequently blocks localhost servers, and this path has zero infrastructure ??it's also what you'll actually use to debug the renderer.

**Validate with `jsonschema` before rendering**, and **strip XML-illegal control characters** (C0 except `\t`/`\n`) from all text ??they reach `python-pptx` and produce a corrupt `.pptx` that PowerPoint refuses to open with no useful error.

## 3. Q2 ??Canvas implementation

**DOM, not `<canvas>`. Do not use Fabric.js.** Absolutely-positioned `<div>`s on a stage div; the triangle as an inline `<svg>` (maps more honestly onto the PPTX triangle than a CSS `clip-path`).

**Why Fabric is the wrong call here**, despite being the obvious answer: it's a `<canvas>` library. Text becomes bitmap-rendered by *Fabric's own* layout engine, so (a) you must reimplement text editing, and (b) Fabric's wrapping metrics become a **third text engine** agreeing with neither the browser nor PowerPoint. For a project whose stated #1 risk is text overflow, adding a third metric model is a bad trade.

The DOM gives you free: `contenteditable` (real text editing, no reimplementation); **real browser metrics in the real Century Gothic** ??the closest possible preview of what PowerPoint will do; and the element *is* the model (`el.style.left`), so serialization is trivial.

Drag/resize is ~150 lines of `pointerdown`/`pointermove`/`pointerup` with pointer capture, or **interact.js** (~10KB) for handles/snapping out of the box. That's the entire library requirement. The scope ??three shapes, a textbox, a title, a colour picker, a font-size control ??is genuinely small. Rotation, if added later, maps cleanly: `shape.rotation` is degrees clockwise, same as CSS `rotate()`.

## 4. Q3 ??Coordinate mapping

`emu = round(model_units * 12700)` ??exact, by construction.

| Editor | PPTX |
|---|---|
| `x, y, w, h` (model units) | `Emu(round(v * 12700))` |
| `size` (font) | `Pt(size)` ??1:1, no factor |
| `stroke.width` | `Pt(width)` |
| `rotation` (deg CW) | `shape.rotation` (deg CW) |
| `#RRGGBB` | `RGBColor.from_string("RRGGBB")` |

**The trap nobody writes down: text-frame internal margins.** PowerPoint defaults to 0.1in left/right and 0.05in top/bottom (91440 / 45720 EMU) ??**7.2 and 3.6 model units**. If the HTML div uses `padding: 0`, the browser wraps text at a *different width* than PowerPoint does, and you get an off-by-one-line overflow that presents as a coordinate bug and will burn a day. Set both sides explicitly and identically:

```python
tf.margin_left = tf.margin_right = Pt(7.2)
tf.margin_top  = tf.margin_bottom = Pt(3.6)
tf.word_wrap = True
tf.auto_size = MSO_AUTO_SIZE.NONE     # there is no autofit
```

**There is no autofit in `python-pptx`.** (`fit_text()` exists but needs a TTF path and is unreliable for CJK ??do not build on it.) Text that overflows stays overflowing.

**But notice this plan turns the project's #1 risk into a non-issue.** `foundation_research` called text overflow the number-one danger precisely because an LLM had to *guess* a character budget. Here a **human sizes the box by eye and sees the text in it**. That's a far stronger defence than any heuristic ??the strongest argument for the user's plan, and worth telling them.

**Verify the mapping once, before building any features.** Calibration slide: four shapes at the exact slide corners, plus a text box of known width and a known string. Export, render via LibreOffice ??PNG, diff against a screenshot of the HTML stage. Converts "I think the mapping is right" into a measured number. One hour.

## 5. Q4 ??Rendering strategy

**All four sub-questions: yes, with one real gap.** Verified against `pptx/enum/shapes.py`:

| Editor shape | `python-pptx` | `prst` | line |
|---|---|---|---|
| Rectangle | `MSO_SHAPE.RECTANGLE` | `rect` | `:586` |
| Circle | `MSO_SHAPE.OVAL` | `ellipse` | `:552` |
| Triangle | `MSO_SHAPE.ISOSCELES_TRIANGLE` | `triangle` | `:381` |

Text in shapes ?? Fill/stroke colours ????**subject to the F4 override**. Century Gothic ??for Latin, **not** for Korean (F3). Drawing onto a real Vieworks slide ??(F1).

**The gap: flow-chart arrows.** `add_connector(MSO_CONNECTOR.STRAIGHT | ELBOW | CURVE, ...)` exists (`shapetree.py:260`), and `begin_connect`/`end_connect` exist (`connector.py:24,125`) ??so connectors can be *glued* to shapes and stay attached when the user later drags a box in PowerPoint. Genuinely valuable; use it.

**But arrowheads are not exposed in the API.** There's no `line.end_arrow`. You must drop to lxml and append `<a:tailEnd type="triangle"/>` to the connector's `<a:ln>`. Small ??but it's the difference between a flow chart and a scatter of plain lines.

More importantly: **the brief's shape list (rectangle, circle, triangle) contains no connector or arrow tool at all.** For "flow charts or block diagrams" that's the missing primitive. Either add a connector tool to Phase 1 scope, or tell the user explicitly that v1 ships without flow-chart arrows. It shouldn't be discovered after the editor is built.

## 6. The one number that is missing

**The geometry of `洹몃옒??8`** ??the divider graphic on layout 3. The manifest records decoration shapes **by name only**, with no box. It's the exact number the user asked for ("match the position of this line with the master slide") and I couldn't extract it, because opening the `.pptx` requires code execution.

**Bound, derived from adjacent geometry I do have:** title placeholder bottom = `0.3715 + 0.5174` = **0.8889in**; every content slide's body textbox top = **1.0202in** (consistent across slides 5, 6, 7, 8, 14, 15, 17, 21 in `exemplar_catalog.md`). So the divider sits in **0.889??.020in**, i.e. **y ??64.0??3.5 model units**. Placing the red line at **y ??68** will be visually right. To get the true value, patch `analyze_pptx_master.py` to emit `decoration_shapes[].emu` ??a ~5-line change ??and re-run.

**Better: make the question disappear.** Render layout 3 to a PNG once (LibreOffice is already a Phase 3 dependency) and set it as the stage's `background-image`. The editor then shows the *real* logo and the *real* gradient divider, the red-line-position problem evaporates, and F2's "the header isn't WYSIWYG" caveat evaporates with it ??true chrome fidelity, for free. Recommend this as the target; the user's flat red line is a perfectly good day-one MVP.

## 7. The scoping consequence the user may not have noticed

**There are now two IRs, and that is correct ??but it is not free.**

| | Semantic IR (existing) | Canvas IR (this plan) |
|---|---|---|
| Emits | `layout_id` + content blocks | absolute geometry |
| Author | the **AI** | a **human** |
| Invariant | *"the AI never emits coordinates"* | the human emits nothing but coordinates |

These are philosophically opposite, and the README's core invariant explicitly forbids the second ??*for the AI*. That invariant is still right and still holds: a human emitting geometry in a visual editor is not the failure mode it was written to prevent (an LLM hallucinating layout). **Keep both. Do not merge them.** They should converge only at the low-level "draw a shape on a slide" primitives.

But the user should hear the cost plainly: **this plan makes `html_adapter.py` and the 30-slide HTML corpus dead code for the visual-editor path.** Phase 1 as previously defined (HTML corpus ??IR ??PPTX round-trip, no AI) is not *extended* by this plan ??it is *replaced*. That may well be the right trade; the visual editor is a better product than a corpus converter. But it's a scope decision with sunk cost, not a free addition, and should be made with open eyes.

## 8. Recommended Phase 1 scope

1. **Calibration slide first** (짠4). One hour. Proves the coordinate mapping before anything is built on it.
2. Patch `analyze_pptx_master.py` to dump `decoration_shapes[].emu` (짠6). Get the real divider y.
3. `canvas_ir.schema.json` + `jsonschema` validation + control-char stripping (짠2).
4. `render_canvas.py`: `add_slide(layout 3)` ??title into placeholder idx 10 ??shapes absolutely, with all four F4 overrides and the F3 font split.
5. The editor: DOM + interact.js, 960횞540 model space, locked title box, red line as stage CSS (짠3).
6. CLI export path first, FastAPI endpoint second (짠2).
7. **Decide the connector/arrow question before building the shape toolbar** (짠5).

## Assumptions and caveats

- **Output location.** The brief specifies no output path, and **writes are denied in this session** ??I attempted `plan_review.md` and was refused, same as the two prior stages. So this analysis exists only in stdout. The orchestrator must persist it; `result.md:14` records a brief being silently truncated by exactly this path, so **verify the saved file ends at 짠8 / "Assumptions and caveats"**.
- **No code was executed.** Every `python-pptx` claim is cited to `file:line` in the installed source ??stronger than memory, weaker than a run. The `<a:ea>` insertion recipe (F3) is schema-correct by construction but unrun.
- **`#FF0032` vs `#DB0032`.** I follow `result.md:18` and treat the user's `#FF0032` override as authoritative for *new* content, with `#DB0032` retained only as the legacy value to detect and overwrite in inherited template content. This plan doesn't change that.
- **I have not seen the divider graphic.** Everything in 짠6 about `洹몃옒??8` is inference from its neighbours' geometry. Its name (`洹몃옒?? = an Office icon/SVG graphic) suggests an SVG rather than a drawn line, consistent with the user's "complex gradient lines".

# Template-Following Mode

Use when the user provides an existing PPTX, asks to follow a presentation, or
attaches a PPTX that is clearly or implicitly a template.

This is the entire template-following mode. Do not run template codegen, do not
build or consume a reusable template registry, and do not rebuild a fresh deck
from palette, fonts, screenshots, or vibes. Use a source slide inventory only:
inspect every source slide, choose source slides for the requested output,
duplicate those source slides, and edit the copied elements in place.

Store every template-following intermediate named in this reference under
`$TMP_DIR`. Only `FINAL_PPTX` may be written outside `$TMP_DIR`.

## Exact Clone/Edit Contract

1. Copy/import the source PPTX.
2. Inspect every source slide render and layout.
3. Create `template-frame-map.json` mapping every output slide to a source
   slide.
4. Build `template-starter.pptx` by duplicating mapped source slides.
5. Apply the requested edits while preserving the source deck's structure and
   styling.
6. Preserve the source template's typography exactly: keep original font
   family, font size, weight, line spacing, paragraph spacing, text insets,
   alignment, and vertical anchor for every edited text box/table cell unless
   the user explicitly asks to restyle or resize. If new copy does not fit,
   shorten the copy, choose a more suitable source layout, or split content
   across another cloned slide; do not silently shrink text to make it fit.
7. Audit inherited placeholders, including `sldNum`, `dt`, and `ftr`, and
   either fill them intentionally or delete them. Treat visible default prompt
   text such as `Slide Number`, `Date`, and `Footer` as an empty inherited
   placeholder; never leave empty PowerPoint placeholders in the final deck,
   even if PNG renders hide them.
8. Export PNGs, layout JSON, and PPTX for QA.
9. Deliver the edited copy and mention the sources cited or used.

The production load/export path is artifact-tool:

```js
const presentation = await PresentationFile.importPptx(await FileBlob.load(sourcePptx));
const pptx = await PresentationFile.exportPptx(presentation);
await pptx.save(outputPptx);
```

Use artifact-tool and headless package tooling only. If artifact-tool cannot
inspect, duplicate, render, or export the source deck, report the blocker. Do
not fall back to a theme-matched rebuild.

## Source Slide Inventory

Before planning final copy, run:

```bash
node "$SKILL_DIR/template_following_scripts/inspect_template_deck.mjs" \
  --workspace "$TMP_DIR" \
  --pptx "<source.pptx>"
```

Review all source slide PNGs, layout JSON files, `template-inspect.ndjson`,
extracted media, font evidence, and `template-manifest.json`. Do not sample only
one or two representative slides.

Then create under `$TMP_DIR`:

- `template-audit.txt`: source structure, reusable slide types, inherited
  placeholders, brand/assets, typography/spacing rules, and insertion contract.
- `template-frame-map.json`: the full source slide inventory and the selected
  source slide for every output slide.
- `deviation-log.txt`: each intentional departure from a copied source slide,
  with reason and affected slides.

`template-frame-map.json` must include:

```json
{
  "outputSlides": [
    {
      "outputSlide": 1,
      "sourceSlide": 3,
      "narrativeRole": "opening thesis",
      "reuseMode": "duplicate-slide",
      "editTargets": []
    }
  ],
  "omittedSourceSlides": [
    { "sourceSlide": 4, "reason": "appendix pattern not needed" }
  ]
}
```

Every output slide requires a `sourceSlide`. Source slides may be reused multiple
times. If a source slide is omitted from the final narrative, record why in the
audit or frame map.

## Build The Starter Deck

After `template-frame-map.json` is complete, run:

```bash
node "$SKILL_DIR/template_following_scripts/prepare_template_starter_deck.mjs" \
  --workspace "$TMP_DIR" \
  --pptx "<source.pptx>" \
  --map "$TMP_DIR/template-frame-map.json" \
  --out "$TMP_DIR/template-starter.pptx" \
  --preview-dir "$TMP_DIR/template-starter-preview" \
  --layout-dir "$TMP_DIR/template-starter-layout" \
  --contact-sheet "$TMP_DIR/template-starter-contact-sheet.png"
```

The starter deck script calls `validate_template_plan.mjs` to validate
`template-frame-map.json` before duplication. For template-following,
`editTargets` must resolve to inherited source elements with `shapeId`,
`shapeIds`, `sourceElementId`, or `sourceElementIds`.
`action: "add"` is rejected by default because it usually creates new content
over copied placeholders. It also never counts as clearing a placeholder:
every inherited structural placeholder, including empty PowerPoint placeholders
with OOXML `<p:ph>` metadata and no visible text in the rendered PNG, must be
filled through the inherited element or explicitly deleted. If the chosen source
slide lacks usable inherited slots, remap to another source slide or report a
blocker.

Use the starter PPTX as the authoring base. Edit copied placeholders, textboxes,
charts, tables, and images by placeholder or resolved element IDs from
`template-inspect.ndjson` whenever possible. Fill inherited component slots; do
not lay a parallel custom design over the copied template slide.

For mapped source slides, do not use `presentation.slides.add()` to build a new
slide. Import the copied/starter PPTX with artifact-tool, edit the existing
duplicated slides, and export through `PresentationFile.exportPptx`.

If a copied slide cannot be edited cleanly, choose a different source slide and
rerun the starter deck script. If no source slide can support the requested
content, report the blocker and the closest source slide options.

## Edit Rules

`editTargets: []` means preserve-only. Do not add narrative text, charts,
tables, images, panels, callouts, or boxes to that slide unless the validated
frame map explicitly allows a new primitive with a bounded zone and reason. New
primitives must not cover inherited template content.

Brand, logo, bumper, divider, separator, chrome, section, and blank slides are
preserve-only patterns unless the source slide contains inherited content slots
that are explicitly filled or deleted. Do not use the large empty brand side of
a bumper as a canvas just because it has visual whitespace.

## Placeholder Clearing Rules

Default every inherited shape, image, table, chart, layout element, master
element, and text object to `keep`. Only rewrite, clear, or delete an inherited
object when it is explicitly classified as `rewrite`, `replace`, or `delete` in
the slide's `template-frame-map.json` `editTargets` or in a separate edit plan
derived from `template-inspect.ndjson` / layout JSON.

Actual placeholders are structural PowerPoint placeholders, such as elements
with resolved placeholder metadata or OOXML `<p:ph>` entries, and source objects
explicitly marked for editing in the map/edit plan. Visible sample content such
as `Name goes here`, `Title goes here`, `Slide Number`, `Date`, `Footer`, and
template instruction notes may be cleared only when that specific object is
classified as a placeholder or authoring note.

Empty structural placeholders still count. If a copied shape has placeholder
metadata but no text, classify it in the edit plan and handle it with
`rewrite`, `rewrite-and-reposition`, `replace`, `delete`, or
`fill-placeholder`. `keep` and `add` do not satisfy placeholder handling because
PowerPoint can show default edit-mode prompts such as `Click to add title` even
when slide PNG renders look clean.

Do not clear text by broad text heuristics. Never run logic equivalent to
`if (text.trim()) shape.text = ""`, and never blank every text-bearing shape on
a copied slide. OpenAI wordmarks, brand text, page chrome, footers, source rails,
section markers, and master/layout furniture may be editable text objects; keep
them unless the edit plan explicitly says to change or delete that exact object.

For data edits:

- Compute first, design second.
- Show formulas or calculation definitions in notes or appendix when useful.
- Rank and conclude from the computed result, not visual intuition.
- Insert the result into an inherited table/chart/metric frame whenever
  practical.

For media edits:

- Verify identity/source before using headshots or logos.
- Never replace missing logos, app icons, mascots, or product UI with
  hand-drawn lookalikes or pseudo-official marks.
- Normalize crops, background treatment, and image size.
- Do not damage existing slide alignment.

For new real-world subjects, products, screenshots, people, places, events, or
evidence, resolve public or official raster assets and swap them into inherited
frames. Do not generate fake screenshots, fake UI, fake logos, fake product
images, fake evidence, or generated approximations of real entities.

## QA

1. Export slide PNGs, layout JSON, PPTX, and the montage with artifact-tool. The
   canonical presentation artifact export writes per-slide renders and a montage
   by default. Set `montage: false` only if a montage is not useful for this
   run.
2. Review each slide against the mapped source pattern, `content.json` or the
   content plan, and rendered output.
3. Check:
   - hierarchy and primary read,
   - text clipping and overflow,
   - font resolution,
   - chart/table legibility,
   - image crop and mask fidelity,
   - source accuracy,
   - spacing rhythm,
   - slide-to-slide pacing,
   - editable token and asset structure,
   - no blanket text clearing or unplanned deletion of brand chrome, logos,
     footers, page markers, master/layout furniture, or source rails,
   - no unfilled inherited placeholders, including `sldNum`, `dt`, `ftr`, or
     visible default prompt text such as `Slide Number`, `Date`, or `Footer`.
     This placeholder check must inspect the final exported PPTX XML, not only
     rendered PNGs. For every `ppt/slides/slide*.xml`, fail QA if any `<p:sp>`
     containing `<p:ph>` has an empty or whitespace-only text body, unless that
     exact placeholder is intentionally filled or deleted in the edit plan. Do
     not pass this gate by overlaying new text boxes on top of empty
     placeholders.
   - required inherited logos and brand marks are visible in the final exported
     PPTX render. If a required logo from the source template is missing after
     import/export, place the authentic extracted source asset onto the slide
     itself.
4. Before delivery, run:

```bash
node "$SKILL_DIR/template_following_scripts/check_template_fidelity.mjs" \
  --workspace "$TMP_DIR" \
  --starter-pptx "$TMP_DIR/template-starter.pptx" \
  --final-pptx "$FINAL_PPTX" \
  --map "$TMP_DIR/template-frame-map.json" \
  --starter-layout-dir "$TMP_DIR/template-starter-layout" \
  --final-layout-dir "$LAYOUT_DIR/final" \
  --edit-dir "$TMP_DIR"
```

If it fails, fix the map or edit inherited elements directly. Do not switch to
overlays, visual rebuilds, Python PPTX mutation, or direct OOXML mutation.

5. Run one slide-scoped QA review per slide for multi-slide decks, then
   integrate the findings into a final polish pass.

# Q3 Post-Worker Review Report

**Verdict: BLOCK**

The scaffold is a useful design exploration, and the generated manifest is byte-linked to the current template, but the proposed `layout_id + ordered blocks -> placeholders` architecture is not implementable against this template as currently analyzed. The template's visual grammar lives primarily in its 23 example slides and their slide-level freeform shapes, not in a set of semantic, fillable slide layouts. The IR, analyzer, renderer contract, and prompts currently assume the opposite.

The project should not enter Phase 1 implementation until the layout strategy is explicitly changed and the IR is normalized around that strategy.

## Scope and evidence

Reviewed:

- `src/aippt/ir/schema.json` and `src/aippt/ir/models.py`
- `src/aippt/analyze_pptx_master.py`
- renderer/adapter/fit/diagram contracts and authoring prompts
- `foundation_research.md`, `README.md`, `reference/layout_manifest.json`, and `reference/layout_catalog.md`
- the current `Vieworks_R&D_ppt_Template.pptx` package, using read-only OPC/XML inspection
- the three HTML corpus files for block-count and limit checks

The manifest source SHA-256 (`[REDACTED]`) and byte count (`1,881,082`) both match the current PPTX. The generated outputs are therefore tied to the checked-in template even though several extracted conclusions are incomplete or misleading.

## Blocking findings

### F-01 — The catalog exposes no layout roles that the adapter requires

**Severity: Critical**

`html_adapter.choose_layout()` requires roles such as `picture`, `table`, `bullets`, `two-content`, `multi-content`, `title-only`, and `section-divider` (`src/aippt/adapters/html_adapter.py:110-139`). The generated catalog contains only:

- `title`
- `blank`
- `custom`

See `reference/layout_catalog.md:17-25`.

The documented fallback also fails: a missing role is supposed to fall back to `bullets`, but no `bullets` layout exists. As a result, nearly every non-title corpus slide will fail layout selection before rendering.

More importantly, the five layouts are not general content layouts:

- two layouts have no placeholders;
- the title layout has two short `BODY` placeholders;
- `1_사용자 지정 레이아웃` has three short `BODY` placeholders clustered in the left half;
- `2_사용자 지정 레이아웃`, used by most example slides, has only one short top-of-slide `BODY` placeholder.

Meanwhile existing slides based on `2_사용자 지정 레이아웃` contain between 2 and 51 top-level shapes (`reference/layout_manifest.json:347-426`). Direct package inspection also found large numbers of slide-level groups and connectors. Those slide-level objects do not become part of a new slide created with `add_slide(layout)`.

**Required decision:** choose one of these designs before renderer implementation:

1. Refactor the PPTX template so its layouts contain real title/body/picture/table regions. This best preserves the current invariant.
2. Extract deterministic layout recipes from selected example slides, then create new slides from those recipes. Geometry still comes from the template analysis, not from the model.
3. Clone designated exemplar slides and replace named content slots. This preserves the current appearance most closely but requires a tested slide-cloning/relationship strategy.

The current design—adding a layout and expecting the example slide's freeform composition to appear—will not work.

### F-02 — The title contract is incompatible with every analyzed layout

**Severity: Critical**

The schema says `slide.title` fills a `TITLE` placeholder (`src/aippt/ir/schema.json:61-65`), and `fill_title()` is specified to use `slide.shapes.title` (`src/aippt/render/renderer.py:119-127`). However, every extracted placeholder is type `BODY`; the title layout itself has `BODY` idx 10 and idx 11 (`reference/layout_manifest.json:59-130`).

`slide.shapes.title` returns `None` when a slide has no title placeholder, as documented by python-pptx. Therefore the specified renderer cannot fill a title on this template. The catalog also reports these title/subtitle boxes as one-bullet body budgets (`reference/layout_catalog.md:32-35`), which is semantically wrong and will confuse both the model and the fitter.

**Required change:** the manifest needs explicit semantic slot metadata independent of `PP_PLACEHOLDER_TYPE`, for example `slot_id: title`, `slot_id: subtitle`, `slot_id: body-main`, with a concrete placeholder idx or deterministic recipe region behind each slot. The IR should target semantic slots, not assume that `TITLE` exists.

### F-03 — Brand accent and font extraction misses the actual layout-level design

**Severity: Critical**

The manifest reports `#DB0032` as `not-found` (`reference/layout_manifest.json:50-53`) because `find_brand_accent()` checks only the theme and slide-master XML (`src/aippt/analyze_pptx_master.py:202-234`). A read-only scan of the PPTX package found `DB0032` in:

- `ppt/slideLayouts/slideLayout3.xml` — layout-level text styling;
- `ppt/slides/slide2.xml` — slide-level text styling;
- `ppt/presProps.xml` — the recent-colour list, which is not itself authoritative design evidence.

The first hit is load-bearing: the accent exists in a layout even though it is absent from the theme and master. The renderer contract has no branch for this state; it only describes theme-slot or master-hardcoded colour resolution (`src/aippt/render/renderer.py:143-146`). Inline code, callouts, and native diagrams therefore have no valid brand-colour source under the current manifest.

Font extraction has the same scope problem. The catalog lists `맑은 고딕` and an empty East-Asian face (`reference/layout_catalog.md:11-15`), but package inspection found layout-level `뷰웍스 B` and `뷰웍스 M` typefaces. The theme also declares the Korean font through `<a:font script="Hang" typeface="맑은 고딕">`; `_typefaces()` reads only `latin`, `ea`, and `cs`, so it incorrectly treats the Korean mapping as absent (`src/aippt/analyze_pptx_master.py:136-144`).

**Required change:** extract effective style per semantic slot, including direct layout run properties, paragraph/list style, theme script-specific fonts, and all relevant colour sources. Record provenance and precedence, not just one guessed value. Scan layout XML for hardcoded brand colours; scan slides only as evidence for exemplar-recipe extraction, not as a global default.

### F-04 — The generated budgets measure header boxes, not usable slide content

**Severity: Critical**

Because most layouts expose only a title-like short `BODY` placeholder, the catalog has no budget for the large body region where tables, diagrams, cards, steps, images, and prose would actually be drawn. Yet the author prompts claim that the catalog measures the target boxes (`prompts/20_slide_fill.md:26-35`). This makes the preventive overflow layer unusable for nearly all block types.

Even for real text placeholders, `content_budget()` uses the full outer width and height (`src/aippt/analyze_pptx_master.py:312-365`) and does not subtract or model:

- text-frame left/right/top/bottom margins;
- bullet indentation and hanging indent;
- inherited `space_before`, `space_after`, and line spacing;
- vertical anchor, rotation, columns, or explicit line breaks;
- level-specific font sizes and paragraph styles;
- neighbouring layout decoration geometry and z-order.

The 10% safety factor is not a substitute for these deterministic measurements. python-pptx exposes text-frame margins and paragraph spacing directly.

**Required change:** compute budgets for normalized semantic regions, using effective inner text extents and level-specific paragraph properties. Heavyweight blocks need area/layout constraints rather than character totals. Keep render-to-image verification as the final authority.

## IR and pipeline findings

### F-05 — Slide cardinality and identity are internally contradictory

**Severity: High**

Three contracts conflict:

- deck metadata says the renderer turns `deck.title` into a title slide (`src/aippt/ir/schema.json:16-33`);
- renderer output must have exactly `len(ir.slides)` slides (`src/aippt/render/renderer.py:76-80`);
- code variants and overflow handling may emit continuation slides (`src/aippt/render/renderer.py:166-170`, `src/aippt/render/fit.py:136-150`).

The HTML adapter also promises one card in -> one slide out (`src/aippt/adapters/html_adapter.py:98-105`). An implicit title slide or renderer-created continuation breaks slide count, stable IDs, source mapping, visual-QA mapping, and deterministic re-runs.

**Required change:** rendering must be one normalized slide in -> one PPTX slide out. Any title slide, code-variant expansion, or continuation split should occur in a deterministic normalization/planning stage that emits a new validated IR with final IDs before rendering.

### F-06 — Ordered blocks cannot be mapped safely to real targets

**Severity: High**

The schema says blocks are assigned to non-title placeholders in order and then to free-floating shapes (`src/aippt/ir/schema.json:72-76`). That is ambiguous when:

- no title placeholder exists;
- multiple `BODY` placeholders have different semantic purposes;
- a block needs a compound region rather than one placeholder;
- a recipe uses slide-level shapes instead of placeholders.

**Required change:** add semantic slot binding. Each block needs a stable `id` and either a `slot_id` or a renderer-resolved placement role. A slot identifier is not geometry and does not violate the “AI never emits coordinates” invariant.

### F-07 — The verified corpus is not fully representable by the schema

**Severity: High**

The corpus contains one ordered process with ten `.step-badge` items (`[REDACTED].html:350-359`), while `steps.items` is capped at six (`src/aippt/ir/schema.json:262-283`). The schema therefore rejects a construct it claims was derived from the corpus.

The seven SVGs are intentionally declared lossy (`foundation_research.md:209-218`), but that means the stated editable 30-slide round-trip goal cannot be clean unless the acceptance criteria explicitly allow those losses or an SVG/raster asset path is added. At least one SVG is substantive architecture/optics content, not decorative chrome.

**Required change:** raise the step cap to cover the measured corpus and let layout budgets decide whether to split. Define an explicit policy for SVG: supported raster/vector asset, pre-authoring conversion, or accepted loss with a testable exception list.

### F-08 — Provenance and repair addressing disappear between outline and IR

**Severity: High**

The outline carries `source_refs` (`prompts/10_outline_storyboard.md:52-67`), but the Slide IR has nowhere to store them. The system prompt requires every factual claim to be traceable, yet the final artifact discards that traceability. Also, `slide.id` is optional and unconstrained, and blocks have no IDs, despite the QA loop relying on stable targeting.

**Required change:** require a patterned, deck-unique slide ID; add block IDs; retain slide/block-level source references and optional source footnotes. Enforce uniqueness in the Python model because JSON Schema cannot express uniqueness by object property.

### F-09 — `models.py` is not a safe validation boundary

**Severity: High**

`Deck.from_dict()` explicitly does not execute JSON Schema validation (`src/aippt/ir/models.py:467-494`). Its manual parsing accepts or mishandles several schema-invalid values: extra properties, invalid enums, excessive item counts, invalid lengths, empty run arrays, and some missing keys. `_parse_block()` also does not enforce all schema limits (`src/aippt/ir/models.py:342-418`). Depending on which adapter constructs a `Deck`, the “normative contract” can be bypassed.

**Required change:** provide one public parse entrypoint that first runs the canonical schema, then performs cross-field/model checks. Keep unchecked construction private or clearly named. Add schema/model conformance tests so both layers reject the same negative fixtures.

### F-10 — `layout_id` is not a stable or unambiguous layout reference

**Severity: Medium**

The analyzer aggregates all masters, while the IR stores only a layout name. Different masters can contain the same layout name. The IR also does not pin the template/manifest SHA, so a regenerated template can silently change geometry under an existing IR while keeping the same names.

**Required change:** identify layouts with at least `master_index + layout_id`, and pin the manifest/template SHA in deck metadata. Prefer a generated stable `layout_key` over a human-editable name.

### F-11 — Asset inputs are under-constrained

**Severity: Medium**

`image.source.value` has no maximum length or media validation; `path` is described as relative but is not constrained (`src/aippt/ir/schema.json:214-235`). This permits oversized base64 payloads and path traversal if a renderer resolves paths naively. Mermaid rendering similarly needs subprocess timeout, input-size limits, and a fixed working directory.

**Required change:** constrain media type and size at runtime, resolve paths under an approved asset root, reject traversal/absolute paths unless explicitly allowed, and isolate/timeout `mmdc`.

## python-pptx gotchas missing or misstated

### F-12 — Table placeholders can be filled directly

**Severity: Medium**

The renderer contract says a `TABLE` placeholder cannot be filled directly and must be deleted after `shapes.add_table()` (`src/aippt/render/renderer.py:161-165`). python-pptx provides `TablePlaceholder.insert_table(rows, cols)`, which replaces the placeholder with a placeholder graphic frame and preserves its relationship/position semantics. The current template has no TABLE placeholder, but the generic renderer contract is still wrong. See the official [placeholder documentation](https://python-pptx.readthedocs.io/en/latest/user/placeholders-using.html) and [table documentation](https://python-pptx.readthedocs.io/en/latest/user/table.html).

### F-13 — Autofit is described too absolutely; `fit_text()` exists

**Severity: Medium**

The research says `TEXT_TO_FIT_SHAPE` “sets a flag. Fits nothing” (`foundation_research.md:316-329`). It is correct that python-pptx does not provide PowerPoint's layout engine and that a flag alone is not a headless proof of fit. However, the API defines `TEXT_TO_FIT_SHAPE` as a consumer-applied shrink strategy, and `TextFrame.fit_text()` performs a local best-fit calculation using a font file or installed font. See the official [text API](https://python-pptx.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api/text.html) and [autofit analysis](https://python-pptx.readthedocs.io/en/latest/dev/analysis/txt-fit-text.html).

`fit_text()` has serious constraints—uniform font assumptions, font discovery, mixed styles, and Korean font availability—so render verification remains mandatory. The design should evaluate it as one deterministic fallback rather than state that no fitting API exists.

### F-14 — Layout-to-master placeholder inheritance is type-based, not idx-first

**Severity: Medium**

`_master_ph_for()` matches master placeholders by idx before type (`src/aippt/analyze_pptx_master.py:435-444`). python-pptx's layout-placeholder implementation maps layout types to a corresponding master placeholder type. An idx collision with a different type can therefore produce incorrect geometry/font inheritance in other templates. See the official [placeholder implementation](https://python-pptx.readthedocs.io/en/latest/_modules/pptx/shapes/placeholder.html).

The current master appears to have no master placeholders, so this did not cause the present output, but it is a generic analyzer defect.

### F-15 — Connector binding is experimental and can produce load errors

**Severity: Medium**

The diagram contract treats `begin_connect()` / `end_connect()` as straightforward (`src/aippt/render/diagrams.py:124-146`). The official [Connector API](https://python-pptx.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api/shapes.html) marks both methods experimental, reliable primarily for rectangular shapes, and warns that invalid connection-point indices can cause a load error.

**Required change:** restrict native diagram nodes to tested rectangular types, map connection points explicitly, save/reopen every generated deck, and render it before acceptance.

### F-16 — Rich-content placeholder replacement invalidates the old object

**Severity: Medium**

`PicturePlaceholder.insert_picture()` and `TablePlaceholder.insert_table()` replace the underlying XML element; the old placeholder proxy becomes invalid. Picture insertion also crops proportionally to fill the placeholder, whereas `shapes.add_picture()` preserves aspect only when one dimension is omitted. The renderer must choose fit versus fill deliberately and retain the returned replacement proxy. See the official [placeholder guide](https://python-pptx.readthedocs.io/en/latest/user/placeholders-using.html) and [shape API](https://python-pptx.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api/shapes.html).

### F-17 — Speaker-notes creation has side effects and can lack a body placeholder

**Severity: Low**

The IR carries notes, but the renderer contract does not define their implementation. Accessing `slide.notes_slide` creates a notes slide if absent; `notes_text_frame` can still be `None` if the notes body placeholder is missing. See the official [Slides/NotesSlide API](https://python-pptx.readthedocs.io/en/latest/api/slides.html). Notes creation and missing-placeholder behavior need explicit tests.

## Documentation and status drift

### F-18 — Repository status documents contradict the generated artifacts

**Severity: Medium**

`README.md:27-59`, `README.md:108-110`, `foundation_research.md:8-32`, `foundation_research.md:222-260`, and `result.md:6-14` all say the analyzer has never run and the layout files are placeholders. The checked-in layout files are now generated and match the current template hash.

This stale status obscures the more important conclusion: the analyzer ran, and its real output disproves the assumed semantic-layout architecture. Refresh the documents after the design decision above; do not merely replace “not run” with “passed.”

## Recommended remediation order

1. **Select the layout strategy**: real semantic layouts, deterministic recipes derived from exemplars, or exemplar cloning.
2. **Extend the analyzer/manifest** for semantic slots, effective style, decoration geometry, template identity, and correct accent/font resolution.
3. **Normalize the IR** so every final slide and block has stable IDs, slot bindings, provenance, and one-to-one render cardinality.
4. **Fix corpus coverage and validation**: ten-step process, explicit SVG policy, schema-first parsing, uniqueness/cross-field checks.
5. **Rewrite the renderer contracts** around the chosen strategy and correct the python-pptx API assumptions.
6. **Regenerate the manifest/catalog and update prompts** only after the manifest semantics are trustworthy.
7. **Implement and run the verification matrix** below before claiming Phase 1 readiness.

## Minimum verification matrix

- Analyzer golden test against this exact template SHA:
  - 1 master, 5 layouts, 23 existing slides;
  - semantic slots correctly identify title/subtitle/body regions despite `BODY` placeholder types;
  - layout-level `DB0032` is found with correct provenance;
  - `script="Hang"` and layout-level `뷰웍스 B` / `뷰웍스 M` are captured.
- Layout/recipe smoke test: materialize every supported semantic role, fill every slot, save, reopen with python-pptx, and verify shape/relationship counts.
- HTML round-trip tests for all 30 cards, including the 10-step slide, tabbed code, three images, tables, and the explicit SVG/simulator loss policy.
- IR negative tests for unknown/duplicate IDs, ambiguous slots, extra properties, invalid enum values, ragged tables, unsafe paths, and oversized payloads.
- Cardinality test proving normalized IR slide count and IDs exactly match output PPTX and rendered PNG order.
- PowerPoint/LibreOffice render verification for every generated slide; no acceptance based solely on XML or estimated budgets.
- Reopen/save integrity test after slide removal, rich-content placeholder replacement, notes creation, connector binding, and relationship-heavy exemplar cloning (if selected).

## Assumptions and caveats

- The brief's POSIX-style `target_repo` value was mojibake; this review treats the directory containing `review_brief.md` as the target repository.
- Python execution was unavailable in this review session, so the analyzer was not re-run and no Python unit tests were executed. Manifest/PPTX identity and the additional template facts above were verified with read-only PowerShell and OPC/XML inspection.
- No slide rendering or visual comparison was performed. The verdict is based on structural contracts, generated manifest data, corpus inspection, and PPTX package contents.
- Line numbers refer to the reviewed working-tree versions on 2026-07-13.
